Markers: DD=circle, AA=square, benign=triangle.\"}}, \"exp33_strategy_prediction_plan002\": {\"hypothesis\": \"Plan_002 checkpoints (主管道, AA OOD ~97%) 的策略预测-检测关联将比 exp32 (exp16 mixed-LLM checkpoints) 更强，因为 plan_002 在 AA OOD 上检测率更高，正确检测样本的策略预测准确率与检测概率的相关性也应更显著。\", \"metrics\": {\"dd_ood\": {\"strategy_acc\": \"0.4924±0.0246\", \"jb_strategy_acc\": \"0.2569±0.0357\", \"macro_f1\": \"0.1965±0.0142\", \"strategy_acc_detected\": \"0.2575±0.0352\", \"strategy_acc_missed\": \"0.1000±0.0000\", \"missed_per_seed\": [0, 0, 1], \"detection_rate_rapport_building\": 0.9958, \"per_class_f1_top\": {\"none\": 0.8666, \"rapport_building\": 0.524, \"gradual_escalation\": 0.2619}}, \"aa_ood\": {\"strategy_acc\": \"0.7112±0.0086\", \"jb_strategy_acc\": \"0.5012±0.0159\", \"macro_f1\": \"0.1888±0.0012\", \"strategy_acc_detected\": \"0.5091±0.0239\", \"strategy_acc_missed\": \"0.2833±0.0360\", \"missed_per_seed\": [1, 7, 1], \"detection_rate_authority_appeal\": 0.9617, \"detection_rate_emotional_manipulation\": 0.9524, \"detection_rate_gradual_escalation\": 1, \"detection_rate_logical_reframing\": 0.9333, \"detection_rate_rapport_building\": 1}, \"vs_exp32\": {\"dd_ood_missed_improvement\": \"31/5/26 → 0/0/1\", \"aa_ood_jb_strategy_acc_improvement\": \"0.068 → 0.501 (+43pp)\", \"aa_ood_detection_rate_improvement\": \"authority_appeal 0.28→0.96, emotional_manipulation 0.52→0.95\"}, \"note\": \"plan_002 checkpoints vastly outperform exp16 mixed-LLM checkpoints in both detection and strategy prediction. Near-perfect detection (DD 0/0/1 misses, AA 1/7/1) enables cleaner strategy-detection analysis.\"}}, \"exp28c_vanilla_supplement\": {\"hypothesis\": \"Vanilla DeBERTa (no DAPT) baseline at N=50 and N=250 will show substantially lower OOD F1 compared to 9-class DAPT, completing the data efficiency curve and confirming that DAPT benefit persists across all training sizes.\", \"metrics\": {\"9class_3seed\": {\"N50\": {\"iid\": \"1.000±0.000\", \"dd_ood\": \"1.000±0.000\", \"aa_ood\": \"0.969±0.030\"}, \"N125\": {\"iid\": \"1.000±0.000\", \"dd_ood\": \"1.000±0.000\", \"aa_ood\": \"0.975±0.016\"}, \"N250\": {\"iid\": \"1.000±0.000\", \"dd_ood\": \"1.000±0.000\", \"aa_ood\": \"0.975±0.029\"}, \"N350\": {\"iid\": \"1.000±0.000\", \"dd_ood\": \"0.978±0.024\", \"aa_ood\": \"0.972±0.027\"}}, \"vanilla_seed42\": {\"N50\": {\"iid\": 0.646, \"dd_ood\": 0.529, \"aa_ood\": 0.234}, \"N125\": {\"iid\": 0.532, \"dd_ood\": 0.244, \"aa_ood\": 0.244}, \"N250\": {\"iid\": 0.987, \"dd_ood\": 0.365, \"aa_ood\": 0.502}, \"N350\": {\"iid\": 1, \"dd_ood\": 0.36, \"aa_ood\": 0.489}}, \"note\": \"Complete data efficiency table. 9-class at N=50 (14% data) AA OOD=0.969, vastly outperforming vanilla at N=350 (100%) AA OOD=0.489. Gap: +48pp.\"}}, \"exp32_strategy_prediction\": {\"hypothesis\": \"Correctly detected jailbreak conversations have higher persuasion strategy prediction accuracy than missed ones, and certain strategy types (e.g., rapport_building, emotional_manipulation) are associated with higher detection rates, because the persuasion head's ability to identify strategies is mechanistically linked to the encoder's discriminative power for downstream detection.\", \"metrics\": {\"dd_ood\": {\"strategy_acc\": \"0.4145±0.0173\", \"jb_strategy_acc\": \"0.1431±0.0260\", \"macro_f1\": \"0.1626±0.0238\", \"strategy_acc_detected\": \"0.1634±0.0200\", \"strategy_acc_missed\": \"0.0758±0.0123\", \"missed_per_seed\": [31, 5, 26], \"detection_rate_rapport_building\": 0.7417, \"corr_det_prob_vs_strat_acc\": \"r~0.45, p<0.001\"}, \"aa_ood\": {\"strategy_acc\": \"0.4604±0.0114\", \"jb_strategy_acc\": \"0.0682±0.0192\", \"macro_f1\": \"0.1145±0.0212\", \"strategy_acc_detected\": \"0.1311±0.0375\", \"strategy_acc_missed\": \"0.0393±0.0142\", \"missed_per_seed\": [63, 41, 66], \"detection_rate_authority_appeal\": 0.2841, \"detection_rate_emotional_manipulation\": 0.5238, \"corr_det_prob_vs_strat_acc\": \"r~0.24, p~0.03\"}}}, \"exp31_lambda_ablation_aa\": {\"hypothesis\": \"Multi-task persuasion loss weight (lambda) affects OOD generalization in E2E fine-tuning: lambda=0 (intent-only) will underperform on hard OOD (ActorAttack) compared to lambda>0, because the persuasion auxiliary signal provides a regularization effect that preserves OOD-relevant features in the encoder. There exists an optimal lambda that maximizes AA OOD without sacrificing IID performance.\", \"metrics\": {\"lambda_0_aa\": 1, \"lambda_0.1_aa\": 0.9904, \"lambda_0.5_aa\": 0.9809, \"lambda_1.0_aa\": 0.9809, \"lambda_2.0_aa\": 0.9439, \"iid_all\": 1, \"dd_all\": 1, \"fitd_all\": 1, \"note\": \"Higher lambda monotonically hurts AA OOD. lambda=0 (intent-only E2E) achieves perfect AA. Persuasion aux loss acts as a distractor during E2E.\"}}, \"exp28b_data_efficiency_supplement\": {\"hypothesis\": \"9-class DAPT maintains robust OOD performance across different random seeds (model initialization), and vanilla DeBERTa shows poor OOD generalization even at smaller training sizes (N=50, N=250), confirming that the data efficiency advantage of 9-class DAPT is not an artifact of a single seed.\", \"metrics\": {\"9class_3seed_N50_aa_ood\": \"0.969±0.037\", \"9class_3seed_N125_aa_ood\": \"0.975±0.019\", \"9class_3seed_N250_aa_ood\": \"0.975±0.035\", \"9class_3seed_N350_aa_ood\": \"0.972±0.033\", \"note\": \"9-class 3-seed complete. Vanilla N=50/250 missing (script crashed float16 dtype in GRU eval). exp28b added seed123+456 for 9-class only.\"}}, \"exp29_inference_efficiency\": {\"hypothesis\": \"DeBERTa+BiGRU achieves orders-of-magnitude faster inference than LLM-based judges while maintaining superior OOD detection accuracy, demonstrating practical deployability for real-time jailbreak detection.\", \"metrics\": {\"deberta_bigru_latency_ms\": 12.12, \"deberta_bigru_throughput\": 82.48, \"deberta_bigru_params_M\": 186.73, \"deberta_bigru_gpu_mem_MB\": 861.5, \"tfidf_lr_latency_ms\": 0.43, \"tfidf_lr_throughput\": 2344.9, \"speedup_vs_qwen3_8b\": 266.4, \"speedup_vs_gpt4o_low\": 165, \"speedup_vs_gpt4o_high\": 412.5}}, \"exp28_data_efficiency\": {\"hypothesis\": \"9-class DAPT maintains strong OOD generalization even at 25% training data (n=50-125), outperforming vanilla baseline at 100% data (n=350), demonstrating high sample efficiency of persuasion-grounded representations.\", \"metrics\": {\"9class\": {\"50\": {\"iid\": 1, \"dd_ood\": 1, \"aa_ood\": 0.99}, \"125\": {\"iid\": 1, \"dd_ood\": 1, \"aa_ood\": 0.99}, \"250\": {\"iid\": 1, \"dd_ood\": 1, \"aa_ood\": 1}, \"350\": {\"iid\": 1, \"dd_ood\": 1, \"aa_ood\": 1}}, \"vanilla\": {\"125\": {\"iid\": 0.53, \"dd_ood\": 0.24, \"aa_ood\": 0.24}, \"350\": {\"iid\": 1, \"dd_ood\": 0.36, \"aa_ood\": 0.49}}, \"note\": \"9-class at 14% data (50 samples) already achieves DD=1.0, AA=0.99 — outperforming vanilla at 100% by 64pp (DD) and 50pp (AA). Sample efficiency entirely driven by DAPT representation quality.\"}}, \"exp30_length_stratified\": {\"hypothesis\": \"9-class DAPT maintains consistent jailbreak detection performance across different conversation lengths (turn counts), with no systematic degradation in shorter or longer conversations.\", \"metrics\": {\"structural_finding\": \"Benign (12-20 turns) and jailbreak (DD 6-10, AA 3-8) have non-overlapping length distributions. Per-bin macro F1 impossible; using per-bin recall instead.\", \"9class_jb_recall_by_bin\": {\"dd_ood\": {\"4-6\": {\"mean\": 100, \"std\": 0, \"n\": 20}, \"7-10\": {\"mean\": 99.4, \"std\": 0.8, \"n\": 60}}, \"aa_ood\": {\"1-3\": {\"mean\": 93.3, \"std\": 6.2, \"n\": 20}, \"4-6\": {\"mean\": 95.6, \"std\": 4.5, \"n\": 38}, \"7-10\": {\"mean\": 100, \"std\": 0, \"n\": 22}}}, \"benign_specificity_10plus\": {\"mean\": 100, \"std\": 0, \"n\": 38}, \"mlm_comparison\": {\"aa_ood_7-10\": {\"mean\": 69.7, \"std\": 33.7}, \"dd_ood_7-10\": {\"mean\": 83.9, \"std\": 22.8}}, \"note\": \"9-class ≥93% recall all bins, no degradation. Short AA (1-3 turns) slightly harder (93.3%). MLM unstable on 7-10 turns (std>22%). Vanilla near-0 recall on all bins.\"}}, \"exp27_mean_pooling\": {\"hypothesis\": \"Mean pooling (no temporal modeling) with end-to-end fine-tuning will match BiGRU E2E on easy OOD (DD) but degrade on hard OOD (ActorAttack), demonstrating that temporal modeling via BiGRU is necessary for detecting multi-turn adaptive attacks.\", \"metrics\": {\"iid_full\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"dd_ood_k1\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"dd_ood_k2\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"dd_ood_k3\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"dd_ood_k5\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"dd_ood_full\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"aa_ood_full\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"fitd_ood_full\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"metric\": \"f1_macro\", \"seeds\": 3, \"early_stop_epoch\": 4}}, \"exp26_frozen_probing\": {\"hypothesis\": \"Frozen 9-class DAPT DeBERTa representations alone carry sufficient OOD generalization signal: a randomly-initialized BiGRU trained on these frozen embeddings should achieve comparable OOD F1 to the jointly-trained plan_002 GRU, because the DAPT pre-training encodes persuasion-strategy structure that transfers across attack types.\", \"metrics\": {\"frozen_probing_mean\": {\"iid\": 1, \"dd_ood_k1\": 0.99, \"dd_ood_k2\": 0.981, \"dd_ood_k3\": 0.972, \"dd_ood_k5\": 0.997, \"dd_ood_full\": 0.997, \"aa_ood_full\": 0.972, \"fitd_ood_full\": 1}, \"frozen_probing_std\": {\"iid\": 0, \"dd_ood_k1\": 0.013, \"dd_ood_k2\": 0.013, \"dd_ood_k3\": 0.026, \"dd_ood_k5\": 0.005, \"dd_ood_full\": 0.005, \"aa_ood_full\": 0.026, \"fitd_ood_full\": 0}, \"e2e_reference\": {\"iid\": 1, \"dd_ood_full\": 1, \"aa_ood_full\": 0.997}, \"delta_vs_e2e\": {\"dd_ood_full\": -0.003, \"aa_ood_full\": -0.025}, \"retention_pct\": {\"dd_ood\": 99.7, \"aa_ood\": 97.5}, \"note\": \"Frozen probing retains >97% of E2E OOD performance. ```text
Good. The "88-90%" claim is supported by registry data (exp14c_prediction_analysis). Now let me compile my findings for the review.

## Summary of findings:

### CRITICAL
1. **Missing bib entry: `zhang2021understanding`** — conclusion.tex L19 cites `\citet{zhang2021understanding}` but this key does NOT exist in references.bib. This will cause a LaTeX compilation error (undefined citation). Severity: critical.

### WARNING
2. **Discussion "Regularization mechanism" paragraph bloat (L19)** — This paragraph expanded from ~2 lines to ~8 lines of dense text. 

The content itself is solid—the Zhang distinction and three non-derivable findings are substantive—but it's trying to pack too much into one Discussion paragraph: mechanism explanation, literature positioning, and novel findings all at once. Under the strict 8-page limit, this risks pushing the paper over.

3. **Grammatical awkwardness in L19** — The em-dash and parenthetical construction creates ambiguity around what "not" is negating. The sentence needs clearer structure to distinguish between the mechanism explanation and the literature comparison.

4. **"88–90% on cross-LLM benign conversations" in L22** — The data comes from a matched 50-sample subset protocol rather than large-scale evaluation, which the conclusion.tex doesn't qualify. The appendix does note this, so it's borderline acceptable.

5. **C1 n inconsistency FIXED** — introduction.tex now correctly states "TOST-confirmed on DD at n=5" instead of "descriptive evidence at n=3", aligning with the Conclusion.

6. **Abstract minor improvement** — Changed "widely deployed safety classifier" to "turn-level safety classifier" for more precision.

7. **Limitations L31** — Added a cross-reference to the length confound analysis in the appendix.

8. **Related work substantially expanded** — The positioning against prior work is much clearer now, with better differentiation and no hallucinated citations.

9. **All 6 protected areas intact** ✓

10. **Em-dash usage concern** — The new paragraph has that dense em-dash and parenthetical structure that matches the AI pattern from the audit checklist, though it's just one instance.

Now I'm preparing the report.
```

[severity: warning] \"88--90\\% on cross-LLM benign\" 缺少限定语**\nconclusion.tex L22 新增 \"and 88--90\\% on cross-LLM benign conversations\"。数据来源已验证（exp14c: MLM=88%, 9-class=90%），但该数据基于 matched 50-sample subset protocol（appendix 中已注明），而 Discussion 正文未加限定。建议加 \"(matched subset)\" 或简单删除此数字——WildChat 68.1% 已足够说明问题。\n\n**4. 1	% experiments.tex — D024 compressed for 8-page limit
2	
3	\section{Experiments}
4	
5	\paragraph{Terminology.}
6	\emph{Cross-attack OOD}: evaluation on attack families unseen during training; benign distribution remains IID throughout (OOD reflects attack-side shift only). $K$: prefix length (number of conversation turns retained). \emph{DAPT}: all Stage-1 adaptations, extending \citet{gururangan2020dontstop} to supervised variants. \emph{Evidence hierarchy}: vanilla $<$ MLM $<$ classification (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}). Throughout this paper, OOD refers to attack-family shift (novel persuasion strategies at test time); benign distribution shift (e.g., non-synthetic user conversations) is evaluated separately in Section~\ref{sec:discussion}.
7	
8	\subsection{Setup}
9	
10	\paragraph{Data.}
11	All synthetic data is generated by Qwen3-8B~\citep{qwen3_2025}.
12	The IID dataset contains 250 Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} jailbreak and 250 topic-anchored benign conversations, split into training (350), validation (74), and test (76) sets with balanced class ratios (e.g., test = 38 jailbreak + 38 benign).
13	Stage~1 (DAPT) uses training + validation conversations; Stage~2 (BiGRU) trains on training, early-stops on validation, and evaluates on test.
14	OOD evaluation pairs attack-specific jailbreak conversations with the 38 IID-test benign conversations: 80 FITD~\citep{fitd2024} (non-discriminative; Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}) + 38 benign = 118 total, 80 Deceptive Delight (DD)~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, and 80 ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} + 38 benign = 118 total.
15	No overlap exists in attack templates across families; benign is IID (same generation pipeline).
16	A paraphrased version of the IID test set (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20) serves as the adversarial robustness benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}).
17	For human data validation, we use ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 jailbreak + 204 benign from real LMSYS user interactions), entirely independent of Qwen3-8B generation.
18	
19	\paragraph{Models.}
20	Seven DeBERTa-v3 variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline and differ only in Stage-1 training (Section~\ref{sec:method}).
21	Our vanilla DeBERTa+BiGRU configuration is architecturally equivalent to DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, differing only in encoder choice (DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\ BERT); it serves as our primary baseline.
22	For cross-encoder validation, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
23	We also compare TF-IDF LR, LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}), and Llama Guard~3~8B~\citep{metallamaguard3_2024} (zero-shot; evaluates last-response safety given conversation context), which detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}).
24	
25	\paragraph{Metrics.}
26	F1 for binary jailbreak detection at prefix $K$=2 and full conversations (full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_prefix}).
27	OOD results report 3-seed mean$\pm$std (seeds 42, 123, 456) for all variants; TOST equivalence tests for 9-class vs.\ scrambled use 5 seeds (42, 123, 456, 789, 1000).
28	For key pairwise comparisons, we report BCa bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals (10,000 resamples over individual predictions pooled across 3 seeds; this assumes cross-seed independence and is exploratory) and paired permutation tests (10,000 permutations). Seed-level F1 values (Table~\ref{tab:seed_level}, Appendix~\ref{app:seed_level}) confirm all pairwise conclusions hold at every seed.
29	Unless otherwise noted, all standard deviations are computed over 3 random seeds using the population formula ($n$ divisor); Bessel-corrected estimates ($n{-}1$ divisor) are $\approx$22\% larger but do not alter any significance conclusions.
30	Stage-1: learning rate $2 \times 10^{-5}$, batch 16, 4 epochs, MLM masking 15\%.
31	Stage-2: two-layer MLP, 20 epochs.
32	
33	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
34	\subsection{Cross-Attack Generalization}
35	
```

### Tool result

_source line 1168 · 2026-05-21T21:51:19.265Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_014ZXfbgRxF15gDVULpHeAZY`</summary>

```text
1	% abstract.tex — D048 deep writing optimization
2	\begin{abstract}
3	Detecting unseen multi-turn jailbreaks requires domain adaptation, but the form of adaptation matters.
4	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, and existing defenses fail to generalize across attack families: Llama Guard~3, a widely deployed turn-level safety classifier, detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation.
5	We isolate the factors behind cross-attack generalization on synthetic multi-turn data through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125 to 355M parameters) decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and granularity on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families and two training compositions.
6	On single-source synthetic data, continued pretraining closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels. This effectiveness is condition-dependent: on mixed-LLM training data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation remains the strongest variant (DD OOD F1\,=\,0.818 vs.\ 0.291 without adaptation).
7	Across three architectures (125--355M), the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks with scale, though architecture and pretraining differences prevent isolating scale as the sole factor.
8	Classification supervision is associated with substantially lower prediction variance on synthetic evaluations (observed across 3 seeds), with limited sensitivity to label correctness, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data.
9	These findings suggest classification DAPT provides robustness through optimization regularization, not semantic feature learning.
10	\end{abstract}
11	
```

### Tool result

_source line 1170 · 2026-05-21T21:51:19.843Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019ygcWfMyZTtpJnv9t2e6Vy`</summary>

```text
1	% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	
5	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.
6	
7	Existing defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}
8	
9	Pretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\ DeepContext) drops from F1\,=\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.
10	
11	Our pipeline, \textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants systematically vary training objectives while sharing the same two-stage pipeline (Section~\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.
12	
13	\begin{figure}[t]
14	\centering
15	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}
16	\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}
17	\label{fig:architecture}
18	\end{figure}
19	
20	Controlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders, classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant on synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\,=\,0.289 $\approx$ vanilla) while classification DAPT maintains 0.818; RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision is associated with substantially lower prediction variance on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}).
21	
22	Our contributions:
23	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
24	  \item \textbf{Systematic decomposition identifying the most robust adaptation and three supplementary negative results.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations on synthetic multi-turn data shows classification-based DAPT is the most robust variant; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. We report three supplementary findings that challenge common assumptions: scrambled labels perform comparably to correct labels (TOST-confirmed on DD at $n$\,=\,5; $d$\,=\,0.09 on ActorAttack), temporal order is irrelevant ($\Delta < 1$\,pp), and label granularity does not affect detection.
25	
26	  \item \textbf{Architecture- and scale-dependent observation on domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) suggest adaptation need shrinks with model size, though encoders differ in architecture; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate scale. RoBERTa-large handles structurally complex attacks without adaptation (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
27	
28	  \item \textbf{Mechanistic interpretation: optimization regularization, not semantic learning.} Three null results converge: frozen probing near-parity (AA gap 3.1\,pp), label content irrelevance ($d$\,=\,0.09), and temporal modeling irrelevance ($|\Delta| < 1$\,pp) are consistent with classification supervision providing robustness through optimization regularization that stabilizes downstream training. This benefit is condition-dependent (variance reversal on human data) but classification DAPT remains strongest under mixed-LLM training (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}). Continued pretraining is 266$\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\ref{app:data_efficiency}).
29	\end{itemize}
30	
```

101	{\footnotesize $^{*}$9-class encoder with mean pooling replacing BiGRU. $^{\dagger}$Deterministic (no std). $^{\ddagger}$MLM-only IID (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}) is single-seed (42); all OOD values are 3-seed. $^{\P}$Zero-shot; no domain training.}
102	\end{table}
103	
104	A clear \emph{evidence hierarchy} emerges across seven variants (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}), ordered by OOD detection at full conversation length on DD:
105	
106	\vspace{2pt}
107	\noindent\hspace{0.5em}{\small vanilla (0.312$\pm$0.026) $<$ topic (0.541$\pm$0.207) $\ll$ wiki-MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) $\approx$ jb-MLM (0.914$\pm$0.115) $<$ scrambled (0.994$\pm$0.005) $=$ 9-class (0.994$\pm$0.005) $\approx$ binary (1.000$\pm$0.000)}
108	\vspace{2pt}
109	
110	\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary mechanism.}
111	MLM bridges $\sim$60\,pp from vanilla (0.312$\pm$0.026 $\to$ 0.914$\pm$0.115) without labels; this supports the DAPT interpretation~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\ 9-class 0.972) confirms that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond lexical overlap.
112	All three classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent detection (F1 = 0.994--1.000); McNemar's ($p > 0.13$) and paired permutation tests ($p \geq 0.40$) provide descriptive evidence suggesting equivalence. Extended to $n$=5 seeds, TOST (margin = 5\,pp) confirms equivalence on DD ($p < 0.001$; Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}); on ActorAttack, the mean gap narrows to 0.5\,pp with TOST approaching significance ($p$\,=\,0.08, SE\,=\,0.026). Cohen's $d$ for 9-class vs.\ scrambled is negligible on both DD ($d \approx 0$) and ActorAttack ($d$\,=\,0.09 at $n$=5, down from 0.59 at $n$=3; Table~\ref{tab:seed_level}). Label quality and granularity are non-factors for detection.
113	Classification supervision is associated with 4--20$\times$ lower cross-seed variance over MLM on synthetic OOD (std 0.005 vs.\ 0.115 on DD; observed range across 3 seeds; formal variance ratio tests are not applicable at $n$=3; cross-seed ensemble yields marginal additional gains, Appendix~\ref{app:ensemble}). This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where classification-supervised variants exhibit the highest variance and pure MLM the lowest (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}).
114	Appendix~\ref{app:tsne} provides t-SNE visualizations confirming that domain-adapted variants produce tighter, more separable embedding clusters than the vanilla baseline.
115	
116	\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}
117	Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\,pp gap), though jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\,pp advantage at early prefixes ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\ 0.911).
118	Perturbation analysis (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}) reveals a sharper domain specificity effect: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\sim$12\,pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\,pp.
119	
120	\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}
121	Topic classification yields DD-Full F1 = 0.541$\pm$0.207; seed 123 falls \emph{below} vanilla (0.252 vs.\ 0.312). Despite using the same jailbreak-domain training data, topic classification performs worse than Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115), which uses out-of-domain text with no labels. A misaligned objective forces the encoder to allocate capacity to irrelevant features and actively destabilizes representations that MLM would preserve.
122	
123	\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on structurally complex attacks.}
124	ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering across longer conversation structures and provides a more challenging OOD benchmark than DD.
125	The evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:
126	
127	\vspace{2pt}
128	\noindent\hspace{0.5em}{\small TF-IDF (0.261) $\approx$ vanilla (0.258$\pm$0.000) $<$ topic (0.625$\pm$0.141) $<$ wiki-MLM (0.686$\pm$0.297) $<$ jb-MLM (0.893$\pm$0.080) $<$ scrambled (0.951$\pm$0.043) $\approx$ 9-class (0.972$\pm$0.026) $\approx$ binary (0.990$\pm$0.008)}
129	\vspace{2pt}
130	
131	TF-IDF collapses from 0.961 (DD) to 0.261 (ActorAttack, precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15). This gap shows that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond bag-of-words.
132	All classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent performance (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\,pp); this extends the DD finding. DAPT provides $\sim$71\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261). Full per-prefix results and per-prefix error analysis in Appendix~\ref{sec:actorattack_full} and~\ref{app:aa_error}.
133	
134	\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}
135	Mean pooling matches BiGRU on both DD and ActorAttack (F1 gap $\leq$ 1\,pp, overlapping CIs; Appendix~\ref{app:temporal}).
136	Turn-order shuffling produces negligible change ($|\Delta| < 1$\,pp) across both DeBERTa-v3-base 9-class (DD $\Delta$\,=\,0.000, AA $\Delta$\,=\,$-$0.009, IID $\Delta$\,=\,0.000) and RoBERTa-large 9-class (DD $\Delta$\,=\,+0.001, AA $\Delta$\,=\,$-$0.007, IID $\Delta$\,=\,0.000), confirming that detection relies on per-turn representations rather than sequential patterns.
137	
138	\paragraph{Summary.}
139	For base-size encoders (125--184M) on our synthetic multi-turn benchmark, domain exposure is the primary mechanism; label correctness, granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity are non-factors. Classification supervision provides a complementary stability benefit (variance reduced 4--20$\times$) without changing the detection ceiling.
140	DD and FITD confirm no regression on easier attacks; ActorAttack provides the discriminative evaluation where lexical methods fail.
141	Detection is stable across conversation lengths (Appendix~\ref{app:length}), near-saturates at 50 conversations (Appendix~\ref{app:data_efficiency}), and is insensitive to auxiliary loss weight (Appendix~\ref{app:lambda}).
142

Label quality and granularity are non-factors for detection.\nClassification supervision is associated with 4--20$\\times$ lower cross-seed variance over MLM on synthetic OOD (std 0.005 vs.\\ 0.115 on DD; observed range across 3 seeds; formal variance ratio tests are not applicable at $n$=3; cross-seed ensemble yields marginal additional gains, Appendix~\\ref{app:ensemble}). This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where classification-supervised variants exhibit the highest variance and pure MLM the lowest (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\nAppendix~\\ref{app:tsne} provides t-SNE visualizations confirming that domain-adapted variants produce tighter, more separable embedding clusters than the vanilla baseline.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}\nWikipedia MLM (0.904$\\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\\,pp gap), though jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\\,pp advantage at early prefixes ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\\ 0.911).\nPerturbation analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:perturbation}) reveals a sharper domain specificity effect: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\\sim$12\\,pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}\nTopic classification yields DD-Full F1 = 0.541$\\pm$0.207; seed 123 falls \\emph{below} vanilla (0.252 vs.\\ 0.312). A misaligned objective forces the encoder to allocate capacity to irrelevant features and actively destabilizes representations that MLM would preserve.\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on structurally complex attacks.}\nActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering across longer conversation structures and provides a more challenging OOD benchmark than DD.\nThe evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\hspace{0.5em}{\\small TF-IDF (0.261) $\\approx$ vanilla (0.258$\\pm$0.000) $<$ topic (0.625$\\pm$0.141) $<$ wiki-MLM (0.686$\\pm$0.297) $<$ jb-MLM (0.893$\\pm$0.080) $<$ scrambled (0.951$\\pm$0.043) $\\approx$ 9-class (0.972$\\pm$0.026) $\\approx$ binary (0.990$\\pm$0.008)}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\nTF-IDF collapses from 0.961 (DD) to 0.261 (ActorAttack, precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15). This gap shows that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond bag-of-words.\nAll classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent performance (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\\,pp); this extends the DD finding. DAPT provides $\\sim$71\\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261). Full per-prefix results and per-prefix error analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:actorattack_full} and~\\ref{app:aa_error}.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}\nMean pooling matches BiGRU on both DD and ActorAttack (F1 gap $\\leq$ 1\\,pp, overlapping CIs; Appendix~\\ref{app:temporal}).\nTurn-order shuffling produces negligible change ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp) across both DeBERTa-v3-base 9-class (DD $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000, AA $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.009, IID $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000) and RoBERTa-large 9-class (DD $\\Delta$\\,=\\,+0.001, AA $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.007, IID $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000), confirming that detection relies on per-turn representations rather than sequential patterns.\n\n\\paragraph{Summary.}\nFor base-size encoders (125--184M) on our synthetic multi-turn benchmark, domain exposure is the primary mechanism; label correctness, granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity are non-factors. 165	\midrule
166	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{RoBERTa-large (355M)}} \\[2pt]
167	Vanilla & .927$\pm$.036 & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} \\
168	MLM & .884$\pm$.065 & .874$\pm$.014 \\
169	9-class & \textbf{.987$\pm$.004} & .946$\pm$.057 \\

173	
174	To test whether the evidence hierarchy depends on encoder choice, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}).
175	On DD, RoBERTa-base vanilla already achieves 0.981$\pm$0.026 (vs.\ DeBERTa 0.312$\pm$0.026), so DAPT adds only 1.3\,pp. ActorAttack exposes this as DD's simplicity: RoBERTa-base vanilla collapses to 0.466$\pm$0.163, a 51.5\,pp drop, while domain-adapted variants recover (9-class: 0.939$\pm$0.046).
176	
177	RoBERTa-large (355M) reveals a capacity-DAPT interaction: its effect on ActorAttack reverses (vanilla 1.000 vs.\ 9-class 0.946), while smaller encoders require DAPT to bridge 47--71\,pp. All continued pretraining reduces ActorAttack performance for the largest encoder via representation drift (Table~\ref{tab:roberta_large_lr}), though classification DAPT partially compensates on DD (+6\,pp).
178	
179	Three encoder scales thus reveal a scale-dependent pattern for the \emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all attacks, RoBERTa-base only for complex ones, and RoBERTa-large for none (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}).
180	Classification supervision provides a consistent stability benefit across both base encoders (cross-seed variance: 0.005--0.046 vs.\ 0.026--0.201).
181	A BERT-base-uncased proxy (110M, matching DeepContext's encoder family) without adaptation achieves DD\,=\,0.318$\pm$0.031 and AA\,=\,0.275$\pm$0.027, comparable to vanilla DeBERTa-v3 (Appendix~\ref{app:bert_proxy}), confirming that cross-attack generalization is driven by DAPT rather than encoder architecture.
182	
183	CKA analysis~\citep{kornblith2019cka} and linear probing confirm that DAPT reshapes representations substantially (Figure~\ref{fig:cka_heatmap}), though comparable CKA shifts yield different detection gains across starting points (Appendix~\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).
184	
185	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
186	\subsection{Mixed-LLM Training}
187	
188	All preceding experiments use single-source (Qwen3-8B) data. To test whether the evidence hierarchy transfers when training data mixes LLM sources, we train on 250 Qwen + 250 Llama-3.1-8B conversations (Table~\ref{tab:mixed_llm}).
189	Classification-based DAPT is the only variant that substantially outperforms vanilla: 9-class achieves DD OOD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 (+53\,pp over vanilla 0.291$\pm$0.065), while MLM drops to vanilla level (0.289$\pm$0.101). On structurally complex ActorAttack, the advantage narrows (9-class 0.504$\pm$0.107 vs.\ vanilla 0.389$\pm$0.073, +11.5\,pp) with high seed variance, suggesting that limited per-source volume (250 per LLM) constrains generalization to complex attacks. DD and FITD thus primarily validate the baseline rather than the method; ActorAttack, where lexical methods collapse (TF-IDF F1\,=\,0.261) and vanilla encoders fail (F1\,$\leq$\,0.389 across conditions), provides the discriminative evaluation axis.
190	Mixed-LLM training confirms that classification supervision generalizes across LLM sources where unsupervised pretraining does not; full results in Appendix~\ref{app:mixed_llm}.
191	
192	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
193	\subsection{Human Data Validation}
194	\label{sec:human_data}
195	
196	Since both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).
197	
198	\begin{table}[t]
199	\centering
200	\small
201	\caption{Human data validation on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (macro F1, 3-seed mean$\pm$std). Bold indicates best. Single-turn data from real LMSYS user interactions, independent of Qwen3-8B.}
202	\label{tab:human_data}
203	\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}
204	\toprule
205	\textbf{Variant} & Macro F1 \\
206	\midrule
207	JB-MLM & \textbf{.837$\pm$.013} \\
208	Wiki-MLM & .791$\pm$.093 \\
209	Binary & .705$\pm$.072 \\
210	Scrambled & .616$\pm$.056 \\
211	9-class & .596$\pm$.087 \\
212	Topic & .496$\pm$.042 \\
213	\midrule
214	Vanilla & .413$\pm$.021 \\
215	\bottomrule
216	\end{tabular}
217	\end{table}
218	
219	All DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\,pp (Table~\ref{tab:human_data}), confirming that the adapted-vs.-vanilla gap is not a Qwen3-8B generation artifact: domain exposure on synthetic jailbreak text transfers to human-written attacks.
220	
221	The ordering reverses: JB-MLM leads (0.837$\pm$0.013) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596$\pm$0.087; $p < 0.001$, non-overlapping CIs). ToxicChat is single-turn, so the classification head provides no advantage; validating the multi-turn hierarchy requires multi-turn human data.
222	
223	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
224	\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}
225	\label{sec:perturbation}
226	
227	Under adversarial paraphrasing (BERTScore 0.92), classification-supervised variants achieve zero degradation on IID and OOD DD (Table~\ref{tab:ood_perturbation}) while non-adapted models degrade by $\sim$12\,pp ($p < 0.001$); on ActorAttack, DAPT variants degrade ($\Delta$\,=\,$-$20.4\,pp) but maintain 52\,pp over vanilla (Appendix~\ref{sec:perturbation_full}).
228	
229	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
230	\subsection{Strategy Trajectory Analysis}
231	\label{sec:strategy}
232	
233	The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of known attacks ($\kappa$=0.78, $n$=50; Appendix~\ref{sec:strategy_full}) but degrades to near-random on OOD ($\kappa$=0.126; Appendix~\ref{app:strategy_pred}). Its value is twofold: as ablation infrastructure (scrambled labels produce equivalent detection, confirming label content is non-critical) and as an interpretability tool for characterizing known attacks; detection operates through domain-adapted \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings, not strategy accuracy.

compensates on DD (+6\\,pp).\n\nThree encoder scales thus reveal a scale-dependent pattern for the \\emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all attacks, RoBERTa-base only for complex ones, and RoBERTa-large for none (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nClassification supervision provides a consistent stability benefit across both base encoders (cross-seed variance: 0.005--0.046 vs.\\ 0.026--0.201).\nA BERT-base-uncased proxy (110M, matching DeepContext's encoder family) without adaptation achieves DD\\,=\\,0.318$\\pm$0.031 and AA\\,=\\,0.275$\\pm$0.027, comparable to vanilla DeBERTa-v3 (Appendix~\\ref{app:bert_proxy}), confirming that cross-attack generalization is driven by DAPT rather than encoder architecture.\n\nCKA analysis~\\citep{kornblith2019cka} and linear probing confirm that DAPT reshapes representations substantially (Figure~\\ref{fig:cka_heatmap}), though comparable CKA shifts yield different detection gains across starting points (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Mixed-LLM Training}\n\nAll preceding experiments use single-source (Qwen3-8B) data. To test whether the evidence hierarchy transfers when training data mixes LLM sources, we train on 250 Qwen + 250 Llama-3.1-8B conversations (Table~\\ref{tab:mixed_llm}).\nClassification-based DAPT is the only variant that substantially outperforms vanilla: 9-class achieves DD OOD F1\\,=\\,0.818$\\pm$0.096 (+53\\,pp over vanilla 0.291$\\pm$0.065), while MLM drops to vanilla level (0.289$\\pm$0.101). On structurally complex ActorAttack, the advantage narrows (9-class 0.504$\\pm$0.107 vs.\\ vanilla 0.389$\\pm$0.073, +11.5\\,pp) with high seed variance, suggesting that limited per-source volume (250 per LLM) constrains generalization to complex attacks. DD and FITD thus primarily validate the baseline rather than the method; ActorAttack, where lexical methods collapse (TF-IDF F1\\,=\\,0.261) and vanilla encoders fail (F1\\,$\\leq$\\,0.389 across conditions), provides the discriminative evaluation axis.\nMixed-LLM training confirms that classification supervision generalizes across LLM sources where unsupervised pretraining does not; full results in Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_llm}.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Human Data Validation}\n\\label{sec:human_data}\n\nSince both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Human data validation on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (macro F1, 3-seed mean$\\pm$std). Bold indicates best. Single-turn data from real LMSYS user interactions, independent of Qwen3-8B.}\n\\label{tab:human_data}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & Macro F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nJB-MLM & \\textbf{.837$\\pm$.013} \\\\\nWiki-MLM & .791$\\pm$.093 \\\\\nBinary & .705$\\pm$.072 \\\\\nScrambled & .616$\\pm$.056 \\\\\n9-class & .596$\\pm$.087 \\\\\nTopic & .496$\\pm$.042 \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & .413$\\pm$.021 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}), confirming that the adapted-vs.-vanilla gap is not a Qwen3-8B generation artifact: domain exposure on synthetic jailbreak text transfers to human-written attacks.\n\nThe ordering reverses: JB-MLM leads (0.837$\\pm$0.013) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596$\\pm$0.087; $p < 0.001$, non-overlapping CIs). ToxicChat is single-turn, so the classification head provides no advantage; validating the multi-turn hierarchy requires multi-turn human data.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:perturbation}\n\nUnder adversarial paraphrasing (BERTScore 0.92), classification-supervised variants achieve zero degradation on IID and OOD DD (Table~\\ref{tab:ood_perturbation}) while non-adapted models degrade by $\\sim$12\\,pp ($p < 0.001$); on ActorAttack, DAPT variants degrade ($\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$20.4\\,pp) but maintain 52\\,pp over vanilla (Appendix~\\ref{sec:perturbation_full}).\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy Trajectory Analysis}\n\\label{sec:strategy}\n\nThe 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of known attacks ($\\kappa$=0.78, $n$=50; Appendix~\\ref{sec:strategy_full}) but degrades to near-random on OOD ($\\kappa$=0.126; Appendix~\\ref{app:strategy_pred}). Its value is twofold: as ablation infrastructure (scrambled labels produce equivalent detection, confirming label content is non-critical) and as an interpretability tool for characterizing known attacks; detection operates through domain-adapted \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings, not strategy accuracy.\n",
      "numLines": 70,
      "startLine": 165,
      "totalLines": 234
    }

Let me verify the `zhang2021understanding` entry is correct. The paper is indeed:
- Zhang et al., "Understanding Deep Learning (Still) Requires Rethinking Generalization"
- Originally published at ICLR 2017, then the "(Still)" version appeared in Communications of the ACM, 2021, Vol 64, No 3, pp 107-115


- DOI: 10.1145/3446776

This matches the CACM version, which is the right citation for the 2021 key. Now I should double-check that conclusion.tex actually uses `zhang2021understanding` as the citation key.
